And just what are we to make of this story, COIIItaD Reader?
Do we simply dismiss it as the dnmbn ramblings of a bitter, guilt-ridden man?
Would it be better to relegate it to the sbadowy realm of Modem Urban Folklore along with tbe tales of
poodles in inicrowaves, crocodiles in the sewen. and aliens in abduction mode?
Or do we dare afford it the merest hint of cndence...A tacit confirmation of the deep-rooted suspicion some
of us haft tbat some places, some areas, are just not right.
Places where the atmosphere seems forever tainted. Fillecl with the embodiment of a hundred,
not-quite-realised fean and disquiets.
Places where the Hpt is a dull, gbost shroud of grey that cuts dark shadows and stiD darker dreams.
Places where tbe very air bangs heavy with some indefinable burden of grief.
Places where EVIL, in aD its forms and guises, chooses to dwelL..
In the cold light of day that contention may seem dangerously paranoid, but oh. in the black watches of the
mpt it seems so perfectly logical.

Just across the road from 'The Tanyard' is a pub called 'The Britannia Inn'

Tbe history of the pub is mundane and uneventful, although in common with ''Ibe Tan Yard', it's built upon

come to consider tbe following accounts that were fint published in 'DEAD OFNIGHT'# 4.

only be described as 'Demonic Entities.'
Tbe experiences were said to have begun with the sudden appearance of phantom monks in 'John's bedroom
when darkness fell. Initially, these apparitions kept a discreet distance, and indeed they wen barely
discernible as tbey lurked In tbe darkest corners of tbe room.

monotonous low chanting, as the shapes emerged from out of the darkness.

black-cowled figures began to creep ever doser to his bed.

these forms were essentially human-like, there . were also present, other beings, shapeless forms that were

Ebury Lodge was built in 1883, on the crest of a hill (the archetypal                  'Hill House' one might say, with
apologies to Shirley Jackson) overlooking        Ealing, West London.
The site was originally an orchard, and as with the 'The Britannia Inn,'                there appears to have been little
trace     of   anything   untoward   occurring    that   may   bave   acted   as   a   catalyst   for wbat   was   later   to
transpire.-Unless it was the erecting of the house itself.
The fad is though, that over a fifty year period, starting in 1883, a total of twenty people killed themselves,
(another was apparently murdered), in this one building.
And, mark tbls, ladies and gentlemen; all the deaths were caused by people jumping orfalllng.from the top of
the seventyfoot tower at thefront ofthe house.
1be 'suicides' culminated in 1934, with tbe 'murder' of a baby thrown from tbe parapet by s nurse who then
'elected' to take the same route down, killing henelf instantly. Not surprisingly, following this latest tragedy,
the buDding stood empty and feU into a state of disrepair. No one would live there and tbe place acquired an
evil reputation.

Andrew Green, tbe weD-known author and 'Ghost-Hunter,' had cause to visit tbe house, now re-named 16,
Montpelier Place, sometime during the Second World War. Alidrew, then aged fifteen, went along to

was to make sure that tbe structure wasn't damp or could cause any possible damage to the goods that may
be stored there. Being more than aware of the building's local reputation, he asked his son if he . would care to
see his fint haunted house. Andrew, like most teenager's faced with the prospect of adventure, was only to
glad to accept the invitation. His chief interest at the time was photography and he thought it might well
present an ideal opportunity to get in some practice with his camera.
Apart from a mysterious smell of sulphur (the place had stood empty for ten long yean), Mr Green passed the
building as suitable for the council's purposes, although, ommously enough, one of the foremen of the removal

footsteps in empty rooms and tools going unaccountably missing.
Andrew, although intrigued by these events, did not it seems, set any great store by their occurrence, and it

almost claustrophobic atmosphere of the old house, the air seemed especially fresh and invigorating.

cocked one leg over the parapet when he was grabbed by the neck and spun round to safety by his frantic:
father.

was only when he dad asked him to take another look down that a wave of vertigo-induced dilziness washed
over him .


There was no blade of grass below.
Only crazy paving.

whenever a big sporting event comes around. Uttle shops in

      THE TURIN SHROUD'S
    'MIRACULOUS' ESCAPE
Fire-fighters managed to rescue the infamous Turin Shroud
from a raging inferno at the San Giovanni Cathedral in
downtown Turin, thereby adding to the cloth's aura of mystery
and reverence.
Dozens of residents of the city applauded enthusiastically as
the Shroud's saviours carried the silver-and-glass reliquary
holding the sacred linen.
About 200 fire-fighters battled the flames, which were still
burning fiercely three hours after it initially broke out. The
cause of the fire remained a mystery at the time of going to
press.
The 14-foot-long linen has been kept enshrined in the catbedral
since way back in 1578. As it well known, the actual cloth
bears the faint yellowish image of the front and back of a man
with thorn marks on the head. lacerations on the back and
bruises on the shoulders. Though a bunch of radiocarbon tests

     CIA ANNOUNCES 'SPY PLANES' BEHIND
                                   UFO PHENOMENA.
A recent report tn the pages or the dally press went almost unnoticed by the general public, reaturtng as tt dtd, a rather
lame and reluctant admission that more than halt of the UFO sighttngs recorded in the 1950's and 60's could be explained
away as U.S. secret military aircraft.
According to the accounts coming out or America, the dlsc-Uke objects sighted in such large numbers throughout those
two decades, were spy planes flying top-secret reconnaissance missions from bases in Nevada and California at the very
beipt oftbe Cold War.
The aircraft, the Lockheed U-2 and the Loc:kheed SR-71 Blackbird, flew at extremely high alltitudes - the latter capable or
more than 2,000 mph at up to 85,000ft. Little wonder, the military say somewhat condescendingly, that the ordinary man
in the street mistook the unconventional aircraft for somethin& even more exotic.


DupHdty bad been necessary, the CIA reasoned, in the interests or natlorual security.
The Air Force had made misleading and deceptive statements in order to aHay. fears and to protect an extraordinarily
sensitive project. So the pubHc were fobbed otr with those infamous, half-hearted explanations or the sightlngs Weather
                                                                                                                •••


balloons, atmospheric phenomena, swamp eas and the like.
The optimists in the CIA hoped that this (ahem) sHghtly belated admission in documents initiaHy pubHshed on the                    t.
Internet, would serve to dispel the rumoun or extraterrestrial craft visiting this planet, and go some way towards
diacrediting the 'UFO-nuta' who have been a coDJtant source of embarrassment to the Air Force, and just about everyone
                                                                             ·
else in authority.
The realists amongst them realised however, that far from deftuing the issue, it has only added t o the web of Intrigue.
The question that will now be asked Is as pertinent as it is obvious Why, after so many years of fooling the public,
                                                                      ••••


should anyone gtve an ounce of credence to anytlllng the CIA says now?
And hot on the heels or that one, we can ask this, why has the CIA decided to suddenly come _clean about a subject 1n
which it had always previously denied any involvement whatsoever?
